621: tilted-ring fits, we derive the PA and inclination by averaging the
622: individual measurements over the radial range, excluding the innermost
623: (deviant) data point. The resulting inclination ($\ikin \sim63\degr
624: \pm 7\degr$) is larger than both the one derived from the \HI\ disk
625: ($\ihi \sim50\degr$), and the optical inclination ($\iopt\sim35\degr$,
626: \citealt{pildis-1997}). However, an inclination of 50\degr, or even
627: 35\degr\ can be ruled out by our kinematic data. Keeping all
628: parameters except the rotation velocity fixed to their best estimates,
629: we derive the rotation curve of D564-8. The maximum rotation velocity
630: is $\vvf\sim 25\kms$ (see Fig.~\ref{fig:btf:d564-8-rc}).

643: \subsection{D565-5}
644: The WSRT observations of D565-5 do not detect any emission at the
645: reported position and redshift.  We do, however, see strong emission
646: from the nearby galaxy NGC 2903 at the edge of the primary beam. The
647: agreement of the apparent velocity of this emission with that of the
648: \cite{schombert-1997} detection suggests that the latter detection was
649: simply NGC 2903 emission that was picked up with the larger
650: single-dish beam.

===== 11136.tex:727 =====
717: \subsection[D575-5]{D575-5 (Data presented in Fig.~\ref{fig:btf:d575-5})}\label{sec:D575-5}
718: D575-5 is classified as a dwarf irregular at an assumed distance of
719: $7.7 \pm 3.1$ Mpc. Its global \HI\ profile has a Gaussian shape and
720: its \50 is the smallest of our sample (\50\ $\sim 19\kms$ for the
721: Hanning smoothed data cube).  \cite{pildis-1997} estimate the optical
722: inclination to be $\iopt \sim 66\degr$, which is higher than the (beam
723: corrected) inclination of the \HI\ disk, for which we derive $\ihi
724: \sim 50\degr$.  However, neither the velocity field, nor the major
725: axis \emph{pV}-diagram show clear signs of rotation.  We were unable
726: to derive a maximum rotation velocity from either the velocity field,
727: or from the major-axis position-velocity diagram.  The second-moment
728: map shows values of 6--8 \kms\ throughout the entire disk. This
729: suggests D575-5 is close to face-on and that a relatively large
730: fraction of the profile width is caused by turbulence. For the
731: inclination correction of \20\ and \50, we use the inclination of the
732: \HI\ disk, as the appearance of D575-5 rules out the optical
733: inclination of $\iopt \sim 66\degr$, though we note that the observed
734: small velocity range indicates that even this \HI\ value is likely to be an
735: overestimate of the true inclination.
736:

758: 150\arcsec$. This is in good agreement with the inclination of the
759: \HI\ disk ($\ihi \sim 57\degr$), but more face-on than the optical
760: inclination \citep[$\iopt \sim 66\degr$, derived
761: by][]{pildis-1997}. Keeping all parameters except \VROT\ fixed, we
762: determine the rotation curve of D631-7 (see
763: Fig.~\ref{fig:btf:d631-7-rc}). It shows the typical solid-body
764: rotation in the inner parts, but reaches a flat part at $\vvf \sim
765: 58\kms$. Assuming the kinematical inclination we derive $\vpv \sim
766: 53\kms$ from the $pV$ diagram.

778: D640-13 is an Im/Sm type galaxy with an adopted distance of $13.4 \pm 3.4$ Mpc.
779: Its global \HI\ profile has a Gaussian shape. Comparing the size and
780: the shape of the beam with that of the moment maps shows that the
781: galaxy is barely resolved.  This heavily affects the derived
782: inclination of the \HI\ disk ($\ihi \sim 71\degr$) which is much
783: higher than the optical inclination ($\iopt \sim 48\degr$) by
784: \cite{pildis-1997}. We therefore use the optical inclination for our
785: further analysis.  As neither the position-velocity diagrams, nor the
786: first-moment map show clear signs of rotation, only the widths of the
787: \20 and \50 profiles remain as a proxy for the maximum rotation

954: 
955: \subsection{Sources of uncertainties}\label{sec:btf:uncertainties}
956: 
957: 
958: \subsubsection{Uncertainty in $V_{\mathrm{max}}$}
959: The uncertainty in $V_{\mathrm{max}}$ depends on the measurement error
960: in the observed velocity and that in the inclination value.  For the
961: velocity uncertainty we assume that we can measure the velocity with
962: an accuracy of one channel width $(\sim 4 \kms)$.  In addition, we
963: assume that noise in the data or intrinsic variance within the
964: galaxies introduce another channel width worth of uncertainty. The total
965: uncertainty we assume is the quadratic sum of these two terms or $6 \kms$.
966: 
967: For the uncertainty in the derived inclination angle, we distinguish
968: between galaxies with a kinematically derived inclination, and those
969: where we had to use the optical inclination or that of the \HI\ disk.
970: For the former category, we adopt the scatter of the inclination
971: values of the individual tilted-ring models (see middle panels in
972: Figs.~\ref{fig:btf:d500-2-rc}-\ref{fig:btf:d631-7-rc}) as the

===== 11136.tex:963 =====
953: \ref{table:btf:rotcur-params} are not corrected for inclination.

974: \ref{table:btf:rotcur-params}).  The average value of the
975: uncertainties in these kinematically derived inclinations is $6\degr$
976: and is used as the uncertainty in the inclination angles of the
977: remaining galaxies.  The total uncertainty of the
978: (inclination-corrected) maximum rotation velocity is then calculated
979: using Gaussian error propagation.

963: assume that noise in the data or intrinsic variance within the
